Grand-Canonical Typicality
Authors: Cedric Igelspacher, Roderich Tumulka, Cornelia Vogel
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We study how the grand-canonical density matrix arises in macroscopic quantum systems. “Canonical typicality” is the known statement that for a typical wave function Ψ Ψ from a micro-canonical energy shell of a quantum system S weakly coupled to a large but finite quantum system B, the reduced density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }^S_\Psi ={{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}^B |\Psi \rangle \langle \Psi |$$\end{document}ρ^ΨS=trB|Ψ⟩⟨Ψ| is approximately equal to the canonical density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}=Z^{-1}_\textrm{can}\exp (-\beta \hat{H}^S)$$\end{document}ρ^can=Zcan-1exp(-βH^S). Here, we discuss the analogous statement and related questions for the grand-canonical density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}=Z^{-1}_\textrm{gc}\exp (-\beta (\hat{H}^S-\mu _1 \hat{N}_{1}^S-\ldots -\mu _r\hat{N}_{r}^S))$$\end{document}ρ^gc=Zgc-1exp(-β(H^S-μ1N^1S-…-μrN^rS)) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_{i}^S$$\end{document}N^iS the number operator for molecules of type i in the system S. This includes (i) the case of chemical reactions (which requires some novel considerations) and (ii) that of systems S defined by a spatial region which particles may enter or leave. It includes statements about how \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc arises from the density matrix of the appropriate (generalized micro-canonical) Hilbert subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_\textrm{gmc}\subset \mathscr {H}^S \otimes \mathscr {H}^B$$\end{document}Hgmc⊂HS⊗HB (defined by a micro-canonical interval of total energy and suitable particle number sectors) or from typical Ψ Ψ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}Hgmc, as well as statements about the distribution of the (conditional) wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^S$$\end{document}ψS of S, which turns out to be a so-called GAP or Scrooge measure. That is, we discuss the foundation and justification of both the density matrix and the distribution of the wave function in the grand-canonical case. To this end (particularly for the chemical reactions), we also need to extend these considerations to the so-called generalized Gibbs ensembles, which apply to systems for which some macroscopic observables are conserved.

Introduction

We are concerned with quantum analogs of the micro-canonical, the canonical, and the grand-canonical ensemble introduced by Gibbs [10] to classical statistical mechanics. While the micro-canonical and the canonical ones have been discussed extensively in the literature, we cover here the foundations of the grand-canonical one (as well as the generalized Gibbs ensemble of (7) below). Each of the three ensembles has two kinds of analogs in quantum theory: a density matrix and a probability distribution of wave functions (i.e., over the unit sphere in Hilbert space). The density matrices are well known: 1a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}&= Z_\textrm{mc}^{-1} \; 1\!\!1_{[E-\Delta E,E]}(\hat{H}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^mc=Zmc-11[E-ΔE,E](H^)1b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}&= Z_\textrm{can}^{-1} \; \exp (-\beta \hat{H}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^can=Zcan-1exp(-βH^)1c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}&= Z_\textrm{gc}^{-1} \; \exp \Bigl (-\beta (\hat{H}- \mu _1 \hat{N}_{1} - \ldots - \mu _r \hat{N}_{r})\Bigr ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^gc=Zgc-1exp(-β(H^-μ1N^1-…-μrN^r)) are the micro-canonical, canonical, and grand-canonical density matrices of a quantum system with Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}$$\end{document}H^ and, in the last case, particle number operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_{i}$$\end{document}N^i for particle type i=1,… , ri=1,…,r; Z is always the normalizing constant, β β the inverse temperature, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _i$$\end{document}μi the parameter called the chemical potential, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\!\!1_{[E-\Delta E,E]}(\hat{H})$$\end{document}1[E-ΔE,E](H^) is the projection to the energy shell or micro-canonical subspace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_{[E-\Delta E,E]}\subset \mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H[E-ΔE,E]⊂H (i.e., the spectral subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}$$\end{document}H^ for this interval, or the span of all eigenvectors with eigenvalue in this interval) in the Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H. It is assumed in (1c) that2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Bigl [\hat{H},\sum _i \mu _i \hat{N}_{i} \Bigr ]=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[H^,∑iμiN^i]=0,or else \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_{\textrm{gc}}$$\end{document}ρ^gc would not be invariant under the unitary time evolution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\exp (-i\hat{H}t/\hbar )$$\end{document}exp(-iH^t/ħ) and thus could not represent thermal equilibrium (which would at least have to be stationary).

measure, see Sect. 7.1 for the definition). By (4b), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}}$$\end{document}GAPρ^can describes the thermal equilibrium distribution of wave functions and can be viewed as a quantum analog of the canonical ensemble from classical statistical mechanics. For example, photons coming from the sun can be taken to have a GAP-distributed wave function. Among other things, a novelty of our paper is that we determine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}Pgc (see Statements 3 and 4b in Sect. 3).

Here is a brief overview of the other contributions of this paper. While some of our statements are more or less straightforward extensions of well-known results [19, 39], most require novel considerations. In the literature, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can has been derived (i) from a maximum entropy principle, (ii) as the partial trace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}ρ^mc, and (iii) by canonical typicality. As we explain, variant (i) is ultimately not a satisfactory justification. The extension of (ii) and (iii) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc hits obstacles in one of the two main cases in which particle numbers change: chemical equilibrium. It requires a generalization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc that has been called the generalized Gibbs ensemble \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}$$\end{document}ρ^gG. After clarifying how \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}$$\end{document}ρ^gG applies to chemical equilibrium, and after formulating as a “general Gibbs principle” in what sense these density matrices are valid, we formulate and derive general versions of several statements about how, and under which conditions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}$$\end{document}ρ^gG and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc arise as either the partial trace of a generalized micro-canonical density matrix or the reduced density matrix of a typical Ψ Ψ from a generalized micro-canonical subspace. We thereby also obtain a unified treatment of changes in particle number due to either chemical reactions or particles entering and leaving a spatial region. We now explain these issues in the remainder of Sect. 1.

How the Ensembles Arise

The justification of the canonical and grand-canonical density matrices and distributions of wave functions has to do with weakly coupling the quantum system, let us call it S, to another quantum system B that is much bigger than S (but finite) and serves as a bath (reservoir). There is then a precise sense of what we mean by “the” probability distribution on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}^S)$$\end{document}S(HS) (where we write the system as an upper index): this sense is the typical distribution of the conditional wave function [5, 8, 20, 28, 53] (a concept first introduced in Bohmian mechanics [5, 28] but applicable in any version of quantum mechanics); see Sect. 3.2 for the definition and discussion. Put briefly, the conditional wave function of a system S entangled with another system B is the natural way of assigning a wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^S$$\end{document}ψS to S when the wave function Ψ Ψ of the combined system is given: take the configuration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_B$$\end{document}QB of B to be random with the Born distribution and conditionalize on the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_B$$\end{document}QB by setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^S(q_S) = \Psi (q_S,Q_B)$$\end{document}ψS(qS)=Ψ(qS,QB) times a normalizing factor. (More generally, a conditional wave function can also be defined relative to other bases than the position basis.)1

Coming back to density matrices, there are two well-known ways of justifying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can: either as the partial trace over B of the micro-canonical density matrix of S∪ BS∪B, or as the partial trace over B of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Psi \rangle \langle \Psi |$$\end{document}|Ψ⟩⟨Ψ|, where the wave function Ψ Ψ of S∪ BS∪B is a typical unit vector in a micro-canonical subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}^S \otimes \mathscr {H}^B$$\end{document}HS⊗HB (a consideration known as canonical typicality [9, 19, 39]; note that the word “typical” refers to a probability distribution over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}^S\otimes \mathscr {H}^B)$$\end{document}S(HS⊗HB), here either the uniform distribution u on the unit sphere of a micro-canonical subspace or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAPρ^). Since the former density matrix is the average of the latter, the latter argument is the stronger one: it asserts that for most Ψ Ψ, S will appear to have density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can, whereas the former argument yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can for S only on average. These two ways arise as well in the grand-canonical case. However, some considerations specific to this case are required. Specifically, Statements 2a, 2b and 3 in Sect. 3 follow from theorems of Popescu et al. [39] and Goldstein et al. [18], while for Statements 1a, 1b, 4a, 4b, and 5, new approaches are needed.

In particular, our considerations are in line with the “individualist” attitude [14, 21] that a closed system S∪ BS∪B in a pure state Ψ Ψ can display thermodynamic behavior; this attitude has been widely applied in recent years, in particular in connection with the eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH) (e.g., [12, 16, 33, 37, 49]). Also the distributions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}_\textrm{mc},\mathbb {P}_\textrm{can},\mathbb {P}_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}Pmc,Pcan,Pgc arise here from an individual pure state Ψ Ψ (in a way to which the conditional wave function is relevant).

A standard way of arriving at either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc is to maximize the von Neumann entropy5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_\textrm{vN}=-k_\textrm{B}{{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}(\hat{\rho }\log \hat{\rho }) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SvN=-kBtr(ρ^logρ^)(with Boltzmann’s constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_{\textrm{B}}$$\end{document}kB) under the constraint that the expected energy is fixed, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}(\hat{\rho }\, \hat{H})=E$$\end{document}tr(ρ^H^)=E (as well as, in the grand-canonical case, the expected particle numbers, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}(\hat{\rho }\, \hat{N}_i)=n_i$$\end{document}tr(ρ^N^i)=ni). While this reasoning may suggest that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc) represents the knowledge of an observer who knows not more about the state than E (and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_i$$\end{document}ni) (e.g., [25, 65, 1, Chap. 4], [2, Chap. 7]) the reasoning outlined above in terms of S∪ BS∪B provides more [39]: a physical reason why the actual, physical state (of S) is given (approximately) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc); see Sect. 4.1 for further discussion.

Also concerning the use of the micro-canonical distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\textrm{mc}=u_{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc})}$$\end{document}umc=uS(Hmc) or density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}ρ^mc, there are different attitudes (e.g., [2, Sec. 6.8]) in the literature: Sometimes its use is regarded as a “principle of equal a priori probabilities” (e.g., [24, 38, 54]) saying that unless we have further knowledge about Ψ Ψ than that it lies in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}Hmc, it is a rational guess to assign each point on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc})$$\end{document}S(Hmc) equal (subjective) probability. As we elucidate in Sect. 4.1.2, our attitude is different and can be summed up like this: Since it is the nature of thermal equilibrium to behave like most Ψ Ψ, we find the thermal equilibrium behavior by studying the behavior of most Ψ Ψ, where “most” refers to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}umc (or, when appropriate, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}ugmc over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{gmc})$$\end{document}S(Hgmc) as in (8)).

Chemical Equilibrium and General Gibbs Principle

Another aspect in the discussion of the grand-canonical ensemble arises from the fact that it refers to a variable particle number and, already classically, there are two distinct situations in which that comes up: (i) for chemical reactions such as6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A + B \leftrightharpoons C + \delta E \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A+B⇋C+δE(“chemical equilibrium”), and (ii) when particles can enter and leave a system (defined by a region in space). To illustrate the latter case, thermal equilibrium in the absence of external fields then leads to equal particle densities in the region and its complement; in order to have a name for it, we will call it “spatial equilibrium” in contrast to “chemical equilibrium.” Many textbooks (e.g., [29, 47, 54, 61]) focus on spatial equilibrium when discussing the grand-canonical ensemble and omit proper reasoning for chemical equilibrium, or focus on thermodynamic potentials when discussing chemical reactions and omit proper justification from the microscopic physical laws. Here, we provide this connection also for chemical equilibrium, which, to the best of our knowledge, is novel. We suggest that chemical equilibrium is best understood by considering conserved macroscopic observables (such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_A-\hat{N}_B$$\end{document}N^A-N^B and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_C+\hat{N}_A$$\end{document}N^C+N^A for the chemical reaction mentioned above) and the more abstract perspective of the so-called “generalized Gibbs ensemble” [1, (4.6)], [3, 37, 43, 58]7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}= Z^{-1}_\textrm{gG}\; \exp \left( \sum _{k=1}^K \lambda _k \hat{Q}_k\right) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^gG=ZgG-1exp∑k=1KλkQ^k,which we also discuss as it plays, in fact, a key role. It represents the thermal equilibrium ensemble for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_1,\ldots , \hat{Q}_K$$\end{document}Q^1,…,Q^K (and only those) are conserved macroscopic observables,2 assuming they commute with each other. (One of them usually is the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}$$\end{document}H^. Preliminary considerations in this direction can be found in [29, Sec. I.4].) Note that, as a consequence, there is a K-parameter family of thermal equilibrium states with parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1,\ldots ,\lambda _K$$\end{document}λ1,…,λK (which includes the canonical case with K=1K=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_1=\hat{H}$$\end{document}Q^1=H^, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1=-\beta $$\end{document}λ1=-β). General Gibbs Principle. One relevant sense of equivalence of ensembles is that, as conjectured by [3], they yield the same Born distribution for practically all observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{A}$$\end{document}A^ except very specially chosen functions of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k$$\end{document}Q^k. We elucidate in Sect. 4.2 why this is plausible.

Conjecture. Except for very special choices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k$$\end{document}Q^k,15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}\text { is locally equivalent to } \hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^gGis locally equivalent toρ^gmc.We give in this paper a derivation of (15) in the case in which each of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k$$\end{document}Q^k is (approximately) extensive, that is, in which, for any spatial region S with complement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{S}$$\end{document}S¯,16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{Q}_k \approx \hat{Q}_{k}^S \otimes \hat{I}^{\overline{S}} + \hat{I}^S \otimes \hat{Q}_k^{\overline{S}} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Q^k≈Q^kS⊗I^S¯+I^S⊗Q^kS¯,provided S is large compared to the size of a molecule and has moderate surface.4 This is the first of our claims, see Statement 1a in Sect. 3. It includes the grand-canonical density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc as a special case (also for chemical equilibrium, see Sect. 2, but also for spatial equilibrium, where the conserved observables are simply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k=\hat{N}_k$$\end{document}Q^k=N^k).

There are further question about equivalence of ensembles that deserve a deeper discussion, such as how large or small the width Δ EΔE of the energy window [E-Δ E,E][E-ΔE,E] can be chosen, or about the size of fluctuations, which can matter near phase transitions; however, we will not provide such a discussion here.

Approach to Equilibrium

We will discuss the approach (time evolution) toward the grand-canonical and the generalized Gibbs ensemble, both concerning the reduced density matrix and the distribution of the conditional wave function; we show that this approach occurs for every initial wave function if the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}$$\end{document}H^ satisfies the appropriate version of the eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH), see Eq.s (68a) and (68b) in Statement 5. For the reduced density matrix, we also prove a mathematical theorem (Proposition 2 in Sect. 8) that somewhat generalizes the existing results in this direction [15, 37, 42, 50, 51] (which were intended for the canonical density matrix). Concerning the approach to thermal equilibrium of the conditional wave function, no results have been in the literature before for either the canonical or the grand-canonical case.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2, we elucidate the framework that will also cover chemical reactions. In Sect. 3, we collect the main statements that we derive in this paper. In Sect. 4, we compare ours to other derivations. In Sects. 5–8, we justify and discuss these statements. Appendices A– G collect the mathematical proofs.

Use of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc: Chemical Reactions

While our statements in Sect. 3 also apply to spatial equilibrium, their full meaning unfolds itself in the case of chemical equilibrium. Since the latter is not commonly discussed in the way we treat it here, we first set the stage in this section by describing our perspective on the use of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc for chemical reactions. Specifically, we explain how the general Gibbs principle formulated around (10) can be applied here, yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc, and allows us to determine the chemical equilibrium. On that basis, we can then formulate our statements in Sect. 3.

(Note that the 1-particle Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_{1i}$$\end{document}H1i from which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}_i$$\end{document}Fi arises may also involve internal degrees of freedom.)

Remark 1

There may be a (somewhat arbitrary) choice of the theoretical physicist involved in defining which states exactly count (say, in the example of (6)) as states of molecule C as opposed to states of a molecule A and a molecule B [32, Sec. 3]. We assume in the following that such a choice has been made. In the case of ionization (say, A = proton, B = electron, C = hydrogen atom), a natural choice [11] would be to count the bound states (from the discrete spectrum of the relative Hamiltonian) as atoms C and the scattering states (from the continuous spectrum of the relative Hamiltonian) as A+BA+B.⋄ ⋄

Of the Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}$$\end{document}H^, we assume that molecules, when separated by a sufficient (still microscopic) distance, hardly interact. Since the details of the interaction term do not matter much in the end, let us focus for a moment on the part of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}$$\end{document}H^ that does matter, the part we need to write down for calculating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{\textrm{eq},i}$$\end{document}neq,i. A key contribution is the kinetic energy of the center of mass of each molecule; another one the external field (e.g., electric, magnetic, or gravitational) to which each molecule is subject; another one the energy contributions of internal degrees of freedom (e.g., rotational, vibrational, or librational) of each molecule. Another key contribution to the full Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}$$\end{document}H^ comes from the rest energy of each molecule; that is, apart from the relativistic contribution from the rest mass of the particles (which is irrelevant for chemical reactions because electron-positron or quark-antiquark pair creation do not occur), the ground state energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{0i}$$\end{document}E0i of each molecule. Of the full Hamiltonian again,26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{H}= \hat{H}_* + \hat{V}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H^=H^∗+V^,we assume that the interaction term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{V}$$\end{document}V^ is mostly negligibly small5 (namely, except during a chemical reaction) and contains non-zero transition elements corresponding to every reaction (17), but not for any transition that is not of the form (17). That is, we assume that the conserved quantities of (17) are the only macroscopic conserved observables. In detail, using (simple) tools from linear algebra, consider the r-dimensional real space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}$$\end{document}R with axes labeled \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1,\ldots ,n_r$$\end{document}n1,…,nr, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L be the subspace spanned by the L vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu _{\ell 1}-\tilde{\nu }_{\ell 1}, \ldots , \nu _{\ell r}-\tilde{\nu }_{\ell r})$$\end{document}(νℓ1-ν~ℓ1,…,νℓr-ν~ℓr). Any change in particle numbers due to reactions of the form (17) lies in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L, any conserved linear combination of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1,\ldots ,n_r$$\end{document}n1,…,nr is orthogonal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L. (In fact, the deeper reason we allow real rather than integer components in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}$$\end{document}R is that we allow real coefficients in these linear combinations.) Choose a complete set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_1,\ldots ,F_{K-1}$$\end{document}F1,…,FK-1 of conserved linear combinations with27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K=\dim (\mathcal {L}^\perp )+1=r+1-\dim \mathcal {L} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K=dim(L⊥)+1=r+1-dimL(which is equal to r+1-Lr+1-L if the L vectors just mentioned are linearly independent). That is, choose a linear mapping (a (K-1)× r(K-1)×r-matrix) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F:\mathcal {R} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{K-1}$$\end{document}F:R→RK-1 with kernel28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F^{-1}(0)=\mathcal {L} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F-1(0)=L.By the dimension formula29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dim \textrm{kernel}(F) + \dim \textrm{image}(F) = r\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dimkernel(F)+dimimage(F)=r,F has full rank, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{rank}(F)=\dim \textrm{image}(F)=K-1$$\end{document}rank(F)=dimimage(F)=K-1, and for every k=1,… ,K-1k=1,…,K-1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_k(n_1,\ldots ,n_r)$$\end{document}Fk(n1,…,nr) is conserved during every reaction (17). We assume that there are no further conserved macroscopic observables.

Remark 2

If we had chosen F differently, it would not have changed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}$$\end{document}ρ^gG. In contrast, if we had chosen F differently, it would have changed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}ρ^gmc (though presumably not in a very relevant way). We prove both statements in Appendix A.⋄ ⋄

Now we apply the general Gibbs principle of Sect. 1. We obtain that there is a K-parameter family of thermal equilibrium states. (For example in (6), there is a 3-parameter family of thermal equilibrium states; the three parameters can be taken to be the energy (or, for that matter, temperature), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_A-n_B$$\end{document}nA-nB, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_A+n_C$$\end{document}nA+nC.) Distribution of the Wave Function

For the next statement, we need the notion of conditional wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^S$$\end{document}ψS [5, 8, 20], which we briefly explain now. The concept starts from the wave function Ψ Ψ of S and B together and makes use of an orthonormal basis (ONB) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=\{|b_1\rangle ,|b_2\rangle ,\ldots \}$$\end{document}b={|b1⟩,|b2⟩,…} of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}^B$$\end{document}HB (or generalized ONB, such as the position basis); the conditional wave function is the random wave function52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ^S=\mathcal {N} \langle b_J |\Psi \rangle _B\in \mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}^S)\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψS=N⟨bJ|Ψ⟩B∈S(HS),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N is the normalizing factor, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \cdot | \cdot \rangle _B$$\end{document}⟨·|·⟩B is the partial inner product, and the basis vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_J$$\end{document}bJ is chosen randomly with Born distribution,53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}(J=j)=\bigl \Vert \langle b_j|\Psi \rangle _B \bigr \Vert _S^2\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(J=j)=‖⟨bj|Ψ⟩B‖S2.The conditional wave function can be thought of as what the collapsed wave function of S would be after an ideal quantum measurement of the basis b on B [18, Footnote 2].

In the following, the GAP measures play a role. For every density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }$$\end{document}ρ^ on a Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H, the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAPρ^ is a probability measure on the unit sphere of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H whose associated density matrix is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }$$\end{document}ρ^; it is the most spread-out measure for the given density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }$$\end{document}ρ^; see Sect. 7.1 for the definition.

We now answer the question, raised in the introduction, of what \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}Pgc is, the probability distribution of the wave function corresponding to the grand-canonical ensemble. We answer it by providing the typical distribution of the conditional wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^S$$\end{document}ψS. The answer is twofold, as it depends on how the basis b is chosen. In general, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^S$$\end{document}ψS (and its distribution) depends on the choice of b, and while b can be chosen arbitrarily, one is often particularly interested in taking b to be the position (or configuration) basis, for several reasons: because that is the basis used in the original concept [5] of conditional wave function, because of the reasoning of Footnote 1, and because it is the one directly relevant if Bohmian mechanics is true.8 We found it to be too difficult to determine the distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^S$$\end{document}ψS for b the position basis, but we have results involving two kinds of typical bases, which can serve as reasonable simplified models for the position basis; both assume that S is a macro-small spatial region and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=\overline{S}$$\end{document}B=S¯: one result in which b is a typical (i.e., random) ONB, and one in which b is typical among those that diagonalize the conserved operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}Q^kS¯ (respectively, the particle number operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_i^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}N^iS¯). The first result (Statement 3) applies whenever b is unrelated to the joint eigenbasis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}H^S¯ and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}Q^kS¯ (respectively, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_i^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}N^iS¯); the second (Statements 4a and 4b) whenever there is no simple relation between b and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}H^S¯ other than that both b and a suitable eigenbasis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}H^S¯ diagonalize the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}Q^kS¯ (the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_i^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}N^iS¯)—this seems like a reasonable approximation for the position basis.

For any random variable X, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_X$$\end{document}LX (“law of X”) denote its probability distribution.Statement 3. Remark 5

If the general Gibbs principle is more generally valid than under the assumptions of Statement 1a (which is plausible as pointed out in Sect. 4.2), then the expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}Pgc can be simplified further:65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}_\textrm{gc}\approx \bigoplus _{q_1^S,...,q_{K-1}^S} p(q_1^S,\ldots ,q_{K-1}^S) ~ \textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }^S(q_1^S,\ldots ,q_{K-1}^S)}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pgc≈⨁q1S,...,qK-1Sp(q1S,…,qK-1S)GAPρ^S(q1S,…,qK-1S),where66\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p(q_1^S,\ldots ,q_{K-1}^S) = {{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}(\hat{\rho }^S_\textrm{gc}\hat{P})~~\text {with }\hat{P}\text { as in }(60) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p(q1S,…,qK-1S)=tr(ρ^gcSP^)withP^as in(60)and67\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }^S(q_1^S, \ldots , q_{K-1}^S) = \frac{1}{p(q_1^S,\ldots ,q_{K-1}^S)} \hat{P} \, \hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}^S \, \hat{P} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^S(q1S,…,qK-1S)=1p(q1S,…,qK-1S)P^ρ^gcSP^.⋄ ⋄

Statement 4b is actually not a special case of Statement 4a because only certain combinations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_i$$\end{document}N^i are conserved as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k$$\end{document}Q^k, and while in Statement 4a we considered bases diagonalizing the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}Q^kS¯ (i.e., certain combinations of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_i^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}N^iS¯), we consider in Statement 4b bases diagonalizing all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_i^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}N^iS¯. That is because we are interested in the position/configuration basis, which diagonalizes all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_i^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}N^iS¯; on the other hand, Statement 4a is the simpler statement, and can be applicable to general \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_k$$\end{document}Q^k that are not related to number operators.

We also note in passing that while the ⊕ ⊕ symbol for measures means merely, according to its definition (55), the mixture of several measures, something more is true in (59) and (65): the measures are even disjoint and, on top of that, their supports even lie in mutually orthogonal subspaces.

Approach to Equilibrium

The next statement concerns the approach (time evolution) towards thermal equilibrium. As many earlier works such as [16, 33, 40, 60], we take “approach” to mean that for most t≥ 0t≥0 in the long run, the system is in thermal equilibrium. }_\textrm{gG}^S~~(\text {respectively, }\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}^S)\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^ΨtS=trS¯|Ψt⟩⟨Ψt|≈ρ^gGS(respectively,ρ^gcS).Furthermore, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt}
