The version of ETH expressed in (68a)-(68b), due to Srednicki [49], is the one appropriate for microscopic thermal equilibrium (“MITE”) [15]; for macroscopic thermal equilibrium, a different condition would be relevant [15, 16, 46].

Discussion

Comparison to Maximum Entropy Principle

The density matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc are often introduced in the literature as the maximizers of the von Neumann entropy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{\textrm{vN}}$$\end{document}SvN (5) under the constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}(\hat{\rho }\hat{H})=E$$\end{document}tr(ρ^H^)=E (and, if appropriate, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}(\hat{\rho }\hat{N})=n$$\end{document}tr(ρ^N^)=n) (e.g., [1, 12, 27], classically [25]). While it is mathematically correct that they are these maximizers, a derivation based merely on this fact is considerably weaker than the one we have presented, and in fact rather questionable, for at least two reasons as follows.

Differences Between Typicality Reasoning and Maximum Entropy Principle

First, the maximization problem leads to a representation of subjective probability (or the knowledge of an observer), but if statistical mechanics were limited to subjective probability then it would not be justified in most applications; in particular, it could not treat any events that take place in the absence of observers (e.g., the formation of stars before humans even existed). In contrast, there is nothing subjective about \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}^{\overline{S}}|\Psi \rangle \langle \Psi |$$\end{document}trS¯|Ψ⟩⟨Ψ|; that is, we have shown how \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc actually occurs in nature.

Second, it remains unclear why one should constrain the expectation value of a probability distribution or density matrix, even when determining the subjective probability distribution of an observer with limited information. After all, if a person was looking for their car and somehow knew only that its location has latitude \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$50^\circ $$\end{document}50∘ N, then the subjective probability distribution would be concentrated on the circle of latitude \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$50^\circ $$\end{document}50∘ N, rather than including other latitudes in such a fashion that the expected latitude is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$50^\circ $$\end{document}50∘ N. Therefore, even for subjective probability, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}ρ^mc or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}ρ^gmc would be much more relevant; it is only after realizing and using the equivalence of ensembles that they can be replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gc}$$\end{document}ρ^gc. A precise version of this equivalence is provided by our Statements 2a and 2b.

Remark 6

The second issue above was mentioned by Balian [1, Sec. 4.1.2], who suggested that prescribing the expectation value of an observable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{A}$$\end{document}A^ made sense when considering subsystems, of which there are many, and each may have a different value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{A}$$\end{document}A^. However, that seems like mixing up the description and the justification of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can. If, as Balian’s reasoning suggests, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can can only apply to subsystems S, while only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}ρ^mc can apply to full systems, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }^S_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^canS can only be justified as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}^{\overline{S}}\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}trS¯ρ^mc, and the maximizer of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{\textrm{vN}}$$\end{document}SvN for S (under whichever constraints) would be irrelevant unless it agreed with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}^{\overline{S}}\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}trS¯ρ^mc. That is, the reasoning apparently undercuts the use of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_\textrm{vN}$$\end{document}SvN for justifying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can. For us, the consideration of subsystems also plays an important role, for example through the concept of local equivalence of density matrices, but the logic is different in our case, as we do not use subjective probability.⋄ ⋄

Origin of the Uniform Measure in Typicality Reasoning

Another question that arises in this context is whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}ρ^mc (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc})}$$\end{document}uS(Hmc)) itself has the status of subjective probability, given that it simply assigns equal weight to states with energies in [E-Δ E,E][E-ΔE,E]. In other words, didn’t we put in an assumption saying something like “all states in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}Hmc are equally probable”? And isn’t such an assumption simply an expression of insufficient knowledge and a method of guessing in the absence of knowledge?

Our answer is: no. The use of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc})}$$\end{document}uS(Hmc) has a different origin; it has nothing to do with anybody’s knowledge, and it doesn’t amount to making an assumption. Rather, it is a characteristic property of thermal equilibrium that the micro-state (here, Ψ Ψ) behaves like the overwhelming majority of micro-states with the same values of E (and, if appropriate, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_1,\ldots , Q_{K-1}$$\end{document}Q1,…,QK-1). Just as most phase points on an energy surface of a classical ideal gas have a Maxwellian empirical distribution of velocities, also most wave functions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc})$$\end{document}S(Hmc) (relative to the natural measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc})}$$\end{document}uS(Hmc)) look like thermal equilibrium [15, 16] (with certain exceptions [15, Sec. 8]). Therefore, it can be taken as the definition of thermal equilibrium to behave like most wave functions. A Ψ Ψ in thermal equilibrium should for this reason be typical relative to the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc})}$$\end{document}uS(Hmc), whose density matrix is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}ρ^mc. That is, it is the nature of thermal equilibrium that leads to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{mc})}$$\end{document}uS(Hmc) (respectively, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{gmc})}$$\end{document}uS(Hgmc)), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}ρ^mc (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}ρ^gmc).

Remark 7

Typicality reasoning has, apart from successful applications in quantum statistical mechanics (see, e.g., [12, 37] for overviews), also gained growing attention in the philosophical literature. For more details on the conceptual meaning of typicality, as well as its application to other fields in physics see [13, 30, 31, 63, 64].

Plausibility of the General Gibbs Principle

The reasoning in this section is of a more heuristic character than in other parts of this paper: what we present here is a mere plausibility argument.

“Equivalence of ensembles” means that one thermodynamic ensemble can be replaced, in either classical or quantum statistical mechanics, by another; many variations of this theme are known, corresponding to different senses of “equivalence.” We now describe a heuristic consideration that makes it plausible that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}ρ^gmc as in (9) is equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}$$\end{document}ρ^gG as in (7), without referring to a precise sense of equivalence. (A similar consideration applies already to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{mc}$$\end{document}ρ^mc and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{can}$$\end{document}ρ^can.)

It is possible to make this consideration a little bit sharper: A relevant sense of “equivalence” could be that71\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {for macroscopic observables }\hat{A},~~{{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}(\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}\hat{A}) \approx {{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}(\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}\hat{A}) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}for macroscopic observablesA^,tr(ρ^gmcA^)≈tr(ρ^gGA^).The width of the peak will be different for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}pgmc and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_\textrm{gG}$$\end{document}pgG:

\usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{M}$$\end{document}M.⋄ ⋄

To be sure, there are observables, such as72\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 1\!\!1_{[-1/\sqrt{M},-1/M]\cup [1/M,1/\sqrt{M}]}(\hat{Q}_1/Q_1-\hat{I})\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1[-1/M,-1/M]∪[1/M,1/M](Q^1/Q1-I^),that yield ≈ 0≈0 for the narrower peak but ≈ 1≈1 for the wider peak; however, relevant macroscopic observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{A}$$\end{document}A^ should be insensitive to the width of the peak, which supports (71).

Laplacian on the Union of Regions

Statement 1b assumed the condition (48) that the 1-particle Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}_1$$\end{document}H^1 is approximately additive relative to a spatial decomposition in two regions. In this section, we argue that this is a reasonable condition that should be satisfied in practice. To this end, we consider, as a concrete example of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}_1$$\end{document}H^1, the Laplacian on the union of two regions.

So consider two spatial regions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _1,\Lambda _2\subset \mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}Λ1,Λ2⊂R3 bordering on each other along some surface Σ Σ. We argue that the free 1-particle Hamiltonian associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda =\Lambda _1 \cup \Lambda _2$$\end{document}Λ=Λ1∪Λ2 is approximately, though not exactly, block diagonal with respect to the corresponding splitting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}= \mathscr {H}_1 \oplus \mathscr {H}_2$$\end{document}H=H1⊕H2 of the 1-particle Hilbert space, provided the surface Σ Σ is moderate (i.e., its area is not large compared to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{vol}(\Lambda _i)^{2/3}$$\end{document}vol(Λi)2/3; if the space dimension is d, then 2/3 should be replaced by (d-1)/d(d-1)/d).

The argument focuses on the negative Laplacian as an example of a free Hamiltonian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}_0$$\end{document}H^0. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{H}_0$$\end{document}H^0 were exactly block diagonal, then a wave function initially concentrated in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _1$$\end{document}Λ1 would never enter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _2$$\end{document}Λ2, but it does. Here is another way of looking at the issue: the negative Laplacian in a region Λ Λ becomes a self-adjoint operator only if we introduce boundary conditions on the boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \Lambda $$\end{document}∂Λ of Λ Λ; suppose we always use Dirichlet boundary conditions (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi |_{\partial \Lambda }=0$$\end{document}ψ|∂Λ=0) to define the self-adjoint operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _\Lambda $$\end{document}ΔΛ; then it is clear that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(-\Delta _{\Lambda _1})\oplus (-\Delta _{\Lambda _2})$$\end{document}(-ΔΛ1)⊕(-ΔΛ2) (which requires ψ ψ to vanish on Σ Σ) is different from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\Delta _\Lambda $$\end{document}-ΔΛ (which does not require ψ ψ to vanish on Σ Σ). Now the argument for being approximately block diagonal, i.e.,73\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\Delta _\Lambda \approx (-\Delta _{\Lambda _1})\oplus (-\Delta _{\Lambda _2})\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-ΔΛ≈(-ΔΛ1)⊕(-ΔΛ2),considers the discrete Laplacian (for simplicity in 1d) on the lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}εZ with mesh width ɛ >0ε>0 and (for simplicity) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _1= \{x\in \varepsilon \mathbb {Z}:x\le 0\}$$\end{document}Λ1={x∈εZ:x≤0} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _2 = \{ x\in \varepsilon \mathbb {Z}:x>0\}$$\end{document}Λ2={x∈εZ:x>0} (so the surface is moderate); then it notes that the difference between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\Delta _\Lambda $$\end{document}-ΔΛ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(-\Delta _{\Lambda _1})\oplus (-\Delta _{\Lambda _2})$$\end{document}(-ΔΛ1)⊕(-ΔΛ2), i.e., between74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{\varepsilon ^2} \left( \begin{array}{ccc|ccc} \ddots & -1& & & & \\ -1& 2& -1& & & \\ & -1& 2& -1& & \\ \hline & & -1& 2& -1& \\ & & & -1& 2& -1\\ & & & & -1& \ddots \\ \end{array} \right) ~~\text {and}~~ \frac{1}{\varepsilon ^2} \left( \begin{array}{ccc|ccc} \ddots & -1& & & & \\ -1& 2& -1& & & \\ & -1& 2& & & \\ \hline & & & 2& -1& \\ & & & -1& 2& -1\\ & & & & -1& \ddots \\ \end{array} \right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1ε2⋱-1-12-1-12-1-12-1-12-1-1⋱and1ε2⋱-1-12-1-122-1-12-1-1⋱consists of just two entries—very little.

This derivation has parallels with considerations in [6, 19, 23, 29, 47]. However, we do not assume here that relations familiar from thermodynamics are valid.

For the next steps, we need the following mathematical fact, proved in Appendix D.

Proposition 1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}^a,\mathscr {H}^b,\mathscr {H}^c$$\end{document}Ha,Hb,Hc be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Derivation of Statements 3, 4a, 4b: Conditional Wave Function and GAP

Before deriving Statements 3–5, we review the properties of the GAP measure (for a pedagogical introduction see [55]).

GAP Measure

GAP measures can arise in several ways [18, 20]; the one most relevant to us is as the asymptotic distribution of the conditional wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^S$$\end{document}ψS in the setting of canonical typicality (leading to (4b)) and, of course, according to Statements 3–4b. That is, GAP measures are relevant as the distribution of wave functions for Gibbs ensembles.

For any Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H and probability measure μ μ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H})$$\end{document}S(H), the density matrix of μ μ is96\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_\mu = \int _{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H})} \mu (d\psi ) \, \vert \psi \rangle \langle \psi \vert . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^μ=∫S(H)μ(dψ)|ψ⟩⟨ψ|.Note that the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \mapsto \hat{\rho }_\mu $$\end{document}μ↦ρ^μ is many-to-one; there are several measures leading to the same density matrix. For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }$$\end{document}ρ^, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAPρ^ is a particular measure with density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }$$\end{document}ρ^, i.e.,97\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_{\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }}} = \hat{\rho }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^GAPρ^=ρ^.In fact, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAPρ^ is the most spread-out measure with given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }$$\end{document}ρ^ [26]. The name stems from a procedure for constructing it: G stands for Gaussian, A for adjusted and P for projected. Here is the procedure:

We write X∼ pX∼p to express that the random variable X has probability distribution p. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }=\sum _m p_m |m\rangle \langle m|$$\end{document}ρ^=∑mpm|m⟩⟨m| be a density matrix on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H with eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_m$$\end{document}pm and corresponding eigen-ONB \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{|m\rangle \}$$\end{document}{|m⟩}. Moreover, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Z_m)$$\end{document}(Zm) be a sequence of independent complex-valued Gaussian random variables10 with mean 0 and variances98\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} |Z_m|^2 = p_m. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E|Zm|2=pm.Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{G}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}Gρ^ is the distribution of the random vector99\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi ^\textrm{G} := \sum _m Z_m |m\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΨG:=∑mZm|m⟩.Note that while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H})$$\end{document}S(H). However, as the eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_m$$\end{document}pm sum up to 1, we immediately see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}\Vert \Psi ^\textrm{G}\Vert ^2 = 1$$\end{document}E‖ΨG‖2=1.

If we now projected the Gaussian measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{G}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}Gρ^ to the sphere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H})$$\end{document}S(H), the resulting distribution would in general not have density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }$$\end{document}ρ^. In order to obtain a measure with the desired density matrix after projecting to the sphere, we have to adjust the density of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{G}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}Gρ^. More precisely, we define the Gaussian adjusted measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{ GA}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAρ^ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H by100\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text{ GA}_{\hat{\rho }}(d\psi ) = \Vert \psi \Vert ^2 \textrm{G}_{\hat{\rho }}(d\psi ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GAρ^(dψ)=‖ψ‖2Gρ^(dψ).We remark that because of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}\Vert \Psi ^\textrm{G}\Vert ^2 = 1$$\end{document}E‖ΨG‖2=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{ GA}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAρ^ defines indeed a probability distribution on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H.

In the last step of the construction we project \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{ GA}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAρ^ to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H})$$\end{document}S(H). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi ^{\textrm{GA}}\sim \text{ GA}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}ΨGA∼GAρ^. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAPρ^ is the distribution of the random vector101\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi ^{\textrm{GAP}} := \frac{\Psi ^{\textrm{GA}}}{\Vert \Psi ^{\textrm{GA}}\Vert }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΨGAP:=ΨGA‖ΨGA‖.Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi ^{\textrm{GAP}}$$\end{document}ΨGAP is well-defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi ^{\textrm{GA}}\ne 0$$\end{document}ΨGA≠0 almost surely. One can easily see that indeed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_{\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }}}=\hat{\rho }$$\end{document}ρ^GAPρ^=ρ^ and thus this concludes the construction of GAP measures in the finite-dimensional setting. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}$$\end{document}H is infinite-dimensional, a similar construction is possible, see [56] for the details. For other constructions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }}$$\end{document}GAPρ^, see [18].

Another property we use is the continuous dependence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Remark 8

Another reasoning leads to the related result that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_{\psi ^S}$$\end{document}LψS, when averaged over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{gmc})$$\end{document}Ψ∈S(Hgmc), is close to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}^S}$$\end{document}GAPρ^gGS. It is based on the property [20, Sec. 3, Property 3] that “GAP is hereditary,” i.e., that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \in \mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}^S \otimes \mathscr {H}^B)$$\end{document}Ψ∈S(HS⊗HB) has distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }^S \otimes \hat{\rho }^B}$$\end{document}GAPρ^S⊗ρ^B, then for any fixed ONB b of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}^B$$\end{document}HB, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}_\Psi \mathscr {L}_{\psi ^S} = \textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }^S}$$\end{document}EΨLψS=GAPρ^S. The difference to (105) is twofold: First, the equality is exact, not approximate; and second, it concerns the average over Ψ Ψ, while (105) is the case for most Ψ Ψ. For our application, the exactness does not help as it gets lost in other steps. The application here looks as follows: Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}\approx \hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}$$\end{document}ρ^gmc≈ρ^gG. Then the typicality measure for Ψ Ψ is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{gmc})}= \textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}} \approx \textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}} \approx \textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}^S \otimes \hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}^{\overline{S}}}$$\end{document}uS(Hgmc)=GAPρ^gmc≈GAPρ^gG≈GAPρ^gGS⊗ρ^gGS¯ by (43) and (102). By hereditarity, for any ONB b of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}HS¯, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}_\Psi \mathscr {L}_{\psi ^S}\approx \textrm{GAP}_{\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gG}^S}$$\end{document}EΨLψS≈GAPρ^gGS.⋄ ⋄

Derivation of Statement 4a

An ONB diagonalizing certain commuting observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{A}_1,\ldots ,\hat{A}_n$$\end{document}A^1,…,A^n (such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_1^{\overline{S}},\ldots ,\hat{Q}_{K-1}^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}Q^1S¯,…,Q^K-1S¯ or later \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}_1^{\overline{S}},\ldots ,\hat{N}_r^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}N^1S¯,…,N^rS¯) consists of an ONB for each of their joint eigenspaces (which in the relevant cases will have high dimension). Thus, a purely random ONB diagonalizing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{A}_1,\ldots ,\hat{A}_n$$\end{document}A^1,…,A^n consists of a purely random ONB in each of the joint eigenspaces. As a consequence, the random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Derivation of Statement 4b

We apply (112) to n=rn=r and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{A}_i=\hat{N}_i^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}A^i=N^iS¯. Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1^{\overline{S}},\ldots ,n_r^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}n1S¯,…,nrS¯, Ψ 'Ψ′ is GAP-distributed with density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{P}_n \hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}\hat{P}_n/p_n(n_1^{\overline{S}},\ldots ,n_r^{\overline{S}})$$\end{document}P^nρ^gmcP^n/pn(n1S¯,…,nrS¯). (This can be shown.) By the results of [53], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}^{\overline{S}} |\Psi '\rangle \langle \Psi '| \approx \hat{\rho }(n_1^{\overline{S}},\ldots ,n_r^{\overline{S}})$$\end{document}trS¯|Ψ′⟩⟨Ψ′|≈ρ^(n1S¯,…,nrS¯) with probability close to 1. Example 2

As a simple example we consider the case of one particle species (r=1)(r=1). In this situation there is no notion of chemical equilibrium but only the one of spatial equilibrium.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}= \mathscr {H}^S \otimes \mathscr {H}^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}H=HS⊗HS¯ and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_1 = \hat{N}$$\end{document}Q^1=N^ be the particle number operator of the total system. Let Δ N = 0ΔN=0 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}N∈N be an eigenvalue of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{N}$$\end{document}N^, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {H}_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}Hgmc is characterized by the (exact) total particle number and a micro-canonical energy interval. As a by-product, we observe that (66) reduces in this example to (122d) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1^S=n^S=N-n^{\overline{S}}$$\end{document}q1S=nS=N-nS¯ because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{Q}_1=\hat{N}$$\end{document}Q^1=N^, and (67) reduces to (123d). Thus, the calculation above amounts to an independent confirmation of Remark 5 in this case that does not assume the validity of the general Gibbs principle.⋄ ⋄

Derivation of Statement 5: Approach to Equilibrium

Mathematical Tools

For deriving Statement 5, we make use of the following three mathematical propositions.

The first one provides a large part of the first statement in Statement 5: it states that a version of the eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH) suitable for microscopic thermal equilibrium (MITE) implies that the reduced density matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }^S_{\Psi _t}$$\end{document}ρ^tS approaches \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}^{\overline{S}}\hat{\rho }_\textrm{gmc}$$\end{document}trS¯ρ^gmc for every initial state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _0\in \mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_\textrm{gmc})$$\end{document}Ψ0∈S(Hgmc). The MITE-ETH was formulated in Statement 5 as (68a) and (68b) and is formulated in Proposition 2 in a more precise version as (126) and (127).

For the mathematical formulation, we recall the precise meaning of “most” [17, 60]: One says that a statement s(x) is true for (1-ɛ )(1-ε)-most x∈ Xx∈X relative to the normalized measure μ μ on X if and only if124\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu \bigl \{x\in X:s(x)\bigr \}\ge 1-\varepsilon \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ{x∈X:s(x)}≥1-ε.One says that a statement s(t) is true for (1-ɛ )(1-ε)-most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,\infty )$$\end{document}t∈[0,∞) if and only if125\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \liminf _{T\rightarrow \infty } \frac{1}{T} \Bigl | \bigl \{t\in [0,\infty ): s(t) \bigr \} \Bigr | \ge 1-\varepsilon \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lim infT→∞1T|{t∈[0,∞):s(t)}|≥1-ε,where |M| denotes the length (Lebesgue measure) of the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\subseteq \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}M⊆R.

Proposition 2 expresses that for every initial state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _0\in \mathbb {S}(\mathscr {H}_R)$$\end{document}ψ0∈S(HR), for most times t≥ 0t≥0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }^S_{\psi _t} \approx {{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}^B\hat{\rho }_R$$\end{document}ρ^tS≈trBρ^R. The proof can be found in Appendix E. It is an adaption and generalization of the one given in [15, 42, 51] for the case that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_R=\hat{\rho }_{\textrm{mc}}$$\end{document}ρ^R=ρ^mc and the Hamiltonian has non-degenerate eigenvalues and eigenvalue gaps.

Another proposition concerns the commutation of quantifiers. It is helpful to introduce the symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}$$\end{document}⋁| to denote “for most.” If A(x, y) is any statement about objects x and y, then “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall x \forall y:A(x,y)$$\end{document}∀x∀y:A(x,y)” is equivalent to “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall y \forall x:A(x,y)$$\end{document}∀y∀x:A(x,y).” Likewise, “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}x {\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}y:A(x,y)$$\end{document}⋁|x⋁|y:A(x,y)” is equivalent to “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}y {\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}x:A(x,y)$$\end{document}⋁|y⋁|x:A(x,y)” [17, Footnote 7] if both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}x$$\end{document}⋁|x and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}y$$\end{document}⋁|y refer to probability spaces (i.e., normalized measures); the situation is more subtle when talking about most times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,\infty )$$\end{document}t∈[0,∞) because the uniform measure on [0,∞ )[0,∞) is not normalizable. In fact, “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}x {\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}t: A(x,t)$$\end{document}⋁|x⋁|t:A(x,t)” implies that “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}t{\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}x:A(x,t)$$\end{document}⋁|t⋁|x:A(x,t)” but in general not vice versa [59, Footnote 5]. The following proposition provides a sufficient condition for commuting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}t$$\end{document}⋁|t from the left to the right of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textstyle \bigvee \hspace{-2.6mm} | \,\,}x$$\end{document}⋁|x.

Proposition 3

Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X,\mathscr {F},\mathbb {P})$$\end{document}(X,F,P) is a probability space, C>0C>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F:X\times [0,\infty ) \rightarrow [0,C]$$\end{document}F:X×[0,∞)→[0,C] is a measurable function, 0<ɛ <10<ε<1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\delta _x<1$$\end{document}0<δx<1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\delta _t<1$$\end{document}0<δt<1. exists.} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(x,t)¯:=limT→∞1T∫0TdtF(x,t)exists.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\delta _t)$$\end{document}(1-δt)-most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal}
